, without changing the operator substantially. This will be convenient in the lower bound of the energy asymptotics, since there we have to verify an operator Taylor approximation, which will be more convenient to do for the functions
\sqrt{1-x}^{\ \beta_{i}}
than for the functions
f_{i}
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\beta_{0}:=4,\beta_{1}:=3
,
\beta_{2}:=\beta_{3}:=\beta_{4}:=2
,
\beta_{5}:=1
and
\beta_{6}:=0
, and let us define the operators
\widetilde{A}_{N}
and
\widetilde{B}_{N}
acting on
\mathcal{F}_{0}
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{N}:

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{2}\mathfrak{Re}\left[A_{r}\sqrt{1-\mathbb{L}}^{2-r}\right],

(3.6)

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}_{N}:

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{6}\mathfrak{Re}\left[B_{r}\sqrt{1-\mathbb{L}}^{\beta_{r}}\right].

(3.7)

Then, given Assumption
1.1
, there exists a constant
K
such that for all
M\leq N

\displaystyle\pm\pi_{M}\left(U_{N}N^{-1}\!H_{N}U_{N}^{-1}\!-\!\widetilde{A}_{N}\!-\!\widetilde{B}_{N}\right)\pi_{M}\leq\frac{C}{N}\sqrt{\frac{M}{N}}\left(b_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot b_{\geq 1}+1\right).

(3.8)

Proof.

According to Eq. (
3.3
), we have

\displaystyle U_{N}N^{-1}\!H_{N}U_{N}^{-1}-\widetilde{A}_{N}-\widetilde{B}_{N}=\sum_{r=0}^{6}\mathfrak{Re}\left[B_{r}\left(f_{r}\left(\mathbb{L}\right)-\sqrt{1-\mathbb{L}}^{\beta_{r}}\right)\right],

(3.9)

with the functions
f_{0},\dots,f_{6}
from Eq. (
3
). Note that for all
N\geq 2

\displaystyle\pm

\displaystyle\pi_{M}B_{0}\left(f_{0}(\mathbb{L})\!-\!(1-\mathbb{L})^{2}\right)\pi_{M}=\pm\frac{1}{2}\hat{v}_{00,00}\ \pi_{M}\left(f_{0}(\mathbb{L})\!-\!(1-\mathbb{L})^{2}\right)\pi_{M}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}|\hat{v}_{00,00}|\ \sup_{x\leq\frac{M}{N}}|f_{0}(x)-(1-x)^{2}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|\hat{v}_{00,00}|\frac{M}{(N-1)N}.

Furthermore,
f_{r}\left(x\right)=\sqrt{1-x}^{\beta_{r}}+O\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)
and therefore we obtain with Lemma
3.5
and the choice
t=1

\displaystyle\pm\pi_{M}B_{r}\left(f_{r}(\mathbb{L})-\sqrt{1-x}^{\ \beta_{r}}\right)\pi_{M}\leq\frac{C}{N}\sqrt{\frac{M}{N}}\left(b_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot b_{\geq 1}+1\right),

for a constant
C
and
r\in\{1,\dots,6\}
.
∎

4
Asymptotics of the Ground State Energy

We start by making the formal definition of the Bogoliubov Hamiltonian
\mathbb{H}
in Eq. (
1.6
) rigorous in Subsection
4.1
. In the following Subsection
4.2
, we will verify the upper bound in the energy asymptotics in Eq. (
1.8
). We will then discuss the proof of the lower bound in Subsection
4.3
, while the verification of the main technical Theorem
4.12
for the lower bound will be postponed to Section
5
.

4.1
Construction of the Bogoliubov Operator
\mathbb{H}

In the following Lemma
4.1
we will identify the Hessian
\mathrm{Hess}|_{u_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{H}}
, and give a precise definition of the Bogoliubov operator in the subsequent Definition
4.3
. Furthermore, we shall see that the operator
\mathbb{H}
is indeed semi-bounded. In the following let us denote with
\mathrm{dom}\left[A\right]:=\mathrm{dom}\big(\sqrt{A}\,\big)
the form domain of an operator
A\geq 0
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Given Assumption
1.1
, the Hessian of the Hartree energy
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{H}}
at the Hartree minimizer
u_{0}
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Hess}|_{u_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{H}}[z]=z^{\dagger}\cdot Q_{\mathrm{H}}\cdot z+G_{\mathrm{H}}^{\dagger}\cdot z\otimes z+\left(z\otimes z\right)^{\dagger}\cdot G_{\mathrm{H}},

(4.1)

where
G_{\mathrm{H}}:=\frac{1}{2}\hat{v}\cdot u_{0}\otimes u_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{H}_{0}\otimes_{\mathrm{s}}\mathcal{H}_{0}}^{\|.\|_{*}}
is in the closure of
\mathcal{H}_{0}\otimes_{\mathrm{s}}\mathcal{H}_{0}
with respect to the norm
\|G\|_{*}:=\|1_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes(T+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot G\|
, and the operator
Q_{\mathrm{H}}
is defined by the equation

\displaystyle z^{\dagger}\cdot Q_{\mathrm{H}}\cdot z:

\displaystyle=z^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot z+\left(z\otimes u_{0}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot z\otimes u_{0}-\mu_{\mathrm{H}}\,z^{\dagger}\cdot z+\left(u_{0}\otimes z\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot z\otimes u_{0}

for all
z\in\mathcal{H}_{0}\cap\mathrm{dom}\left[T\right]
, with
\mu_{\mathrm{H}}:=u_{0}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot u_{0}+(u_{0}\otimes u_{0})^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot u_{0}\otimes u_{0}
. Furthermore,
Q_{\mathrm{H}}
is non-negative and satisfies
\nu^{-1}(T|_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}+1)\leq Q_{\mathrm{H}}+1\leq\nu(T|_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}+1)
for some constant
\nu>0
.

Remark 4.2
.

By Assumption
1.1
, we know that
\hat{v}\cdot u_{0}\otimes u_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{H}_{0}\otimes_{\mathrm{s}}\mathcal{H}_{0}}^{\|.\|_{*}}
, which follows from the fact that
1_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes(T+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot 1_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes(T+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
is a bounded operator and that
u_{0}\in\mathrm{dom}\left[T\right]
. For such elements
G\in\overline{\mathcal{H}_{0}\otimes_{\mathrm{s}}\mathcal{H}_{0}}^{\|.\|_{*}}
, we have that
G_{\mathrm{reg}}:=1_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes(T+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot G
is an element of
\mathcal{H}_{0}\otimes_{\mathrm{s}}\mathcal{H}_{0}
and therefore we can define for all
z\in\mathrm{dom}\left[T\right]

\displaystyle G^{\dagger}\cdot z\otimes z:=G_{\mathrm{reg}}^{\dagger}\cdot z\otimes\left((T+1)^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot z\right).

In a similar fashion, we define the operator
G^{\dagger}\cdot b_{\geq 1}\underline{\otimes}b_{\geq 1}:=G_{\mathrm{reg}}^{\dagger}\cdot b_{\geq 1}\underline{\otimes}\left((T+1)^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot b_{\geq 1}\right)
.

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

With the help of the embedding
\iota
defined in Eq. (
1.10
), we can express the Hessian as
\mathrm{Hess}|_{u_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{H}}[z]=D^{2}|_{0}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{H}}\circ\iota\right)(z)
, where
D^{2}|_{z_{0}}f(z)
denotes the second derivative of a function
f
in the direction
z
evaluated at
z_{0}
. An explicit computation yields Eq. (
4.1
). Regarding the second part of the Lemma, observe that
Q_{\mathrm{H}}\geq 0
follows from the fact that we can always find a phase
\theta_{z}
such that

\displaystyle z^{\dagger}\cdot Q_{\mathrm{H}}\cdot z=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Hess}|_{u_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{H}}[e^{i\theta_{z}}z]\geq 0.

Furthermore, note that
|v|\leq\Lambda(T+1)
implies
\pm\left(1_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\otimes u_{0}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot 1_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\otimes u_{0}\leq c\ 1_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}
with
c:=u_{0}^{\dagger}\cdot\Lambda(T+1)\cdot u_{0}
and

\displaystyle\pm\!\left(u_{0}\!\otimes\!1_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\right)^{\dagger}\!\cdot\!\hat{v}\!\cdot\!1_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\!\otimes\!u_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\!\frac{1}{2}\left(u_{0}\!\otimes\!1_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\right)^{\dagger}\!\cdot\!|\hat{v}|\!\cdot\!u_{0}\!\otimes\!1_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\!+\!\frac{1}{2}\left(1_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\!\otimes\!u_{0}\right)^{\dagger}\!\cdot\!|\hat{v}|\!\cdot\!1_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}\!\otimes\!u_{0}\leq\!c\ 1_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}.

Hence
Q_{\mathrm{H}}\geq 0
implies
Q_{\mathrm{H}}+1\geq T|_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}+1-(2c+|\mu|+1)\geq T|_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}+1-(1+2c+\mu)(Q_{\mathrm{H}}+1)
, and therefore
(2+2c+\mu)(Q_{\mathrm{H}}+1)\geq T|_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}+1
. Furthermore
T\geq 0
implies

\displaystyle Q_{\mathrm{H}}+1\leq T+2c+|\mu|\leq(1+2c+|\mu|)(T|_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}+1).

∎

Definition 4.3
.

Let the selfadjoint operator
Q_{\mathrm{H}}
and
G_{\mathrm{H}}\in\overline{\mathcal{H}_{0}\otimes_{\mathrm{s}}\mathcal{H}_{0}}^{\|.\|_{*}}
be as in Lemma
4.1
. Then we define the Bogoliubov operator
\mathbb{H}
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}:=a_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot Q_{\mathrm{H}}\cdot a_{\geq 1}+G_{\mathrm{H}}^{\dagger}\cdot a_{\geq 1}\ \underline{\otimes}\ a_{\geq 1}+\left(a_{\geq 1}\ \underline{\otimes}\ a_{\geq 1}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot G_{\mathrm{H}}.

(4.2)

Theorem 4.4
.

The quadratic form on the right side of Eq. (
4.2
) is semi-bounded from below and closeable, and consequently defines by Friedrichs extension a selfadjoint operator
\mathbb{H}
with
\inf\sigma\left(\mathbb{H}\right)>-\infty
. Furthermore there exists a sequence of states
\Psi_{M}\in\mathrm{dom}\left[a_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot(T+1)\cdot a_{\geq 1}\right]\cap\mathcal{F}_{\leq M}
,
\|\Psi_{M}\|=1
, such that

\displaystyle\braket{\mathbb{H}}_{\Psi_{M}}\underset{M\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\inf\sigma\left(\mathbb{H}\right).

Additionally there exists a constant
r_{*}>0
such that for all
r<r_{*}
the operator
\mathbb{H}-r\mathbb{A}
satisfies
\inf\sigma\left(\mathbb{H}-r\mathbb{A}\right)>-\infty
as well, where

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}:=-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(a_{j}-a_{j}^{\dagger}\right)^{2}+a_{>d}^{\dagger}\cdot(T+1)\cdot a_{>d}.

(4.3)

The proof of Theorem
4.4
is being carried out in Appendix
B
. We emphasize that
\mathbb{H}
is degenerate, in the sense that
z^{\dagger}\cdot Q_{\mathrm{H}}\cdot z+G_{\mathrm{H}}^{\dagger}\cdot z\otimes z+\left(z\otimes z\right)^{\dagger}\cdot G_{\mathrm{H}}=0
for any
z
in the vector space spanned by
\{u_{1},\dots,u_{d}\}
, and therefore we cannot directly apply the results in
[
27
]
. We also note that the semi-boundedness of Bogoliubov operators with degeneracies has been verified in
[
14
]
under the additional assumption that
Q_{\mathrm{H}}
is bounded.

4.2
Upper Bound

With the essential definitions at hand, we will derive the upper bound in Theorem
4.6
using the representation of
U_{N}H_{N}U_{N}^{-1}
derived in the previous section. We follow the strategy presented in
[
21
]
, by sorting the operator
U_{N}H_{N}U_{N}^{-1}
in terms of different powers in
b_{\geq 1}
and identifying the zero component as the Hartree energy
Ne_{\mathrm{H}}
defined in Eq. (
1.2
) and the second order component as the Bogoliubov operator
\mathbb{H}
defined in Eq. (
4.2
).

Lemma 4.5
.

Let Assumption
1.1
hold. Then there exists a constant
C
such that

\displaystyle\pm\pi_{M}\left(U_{N}N^{-1}\!H_{N}U_{N}^{-1}-e_{\mathrm{H}}-N^{-1}\mathbb{H}\right)\pi_{M}\leq C\ \left(\frac{M}{N}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\ \left(1+a_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot(T+1)\cdot a_{\geq 1}\right)

for all
M\leq N
.

While Lemma
4.5
will be useful for proving the upper bound in Theorem
4.6
, it is insufficient for proving the corresponding lower bound. This is due to the fact that Bose–Einstein condensation only provides the rough a priori information
M=o(N)
, see also the proof of Theorem
4.13
.

Proof.

Observe that
u_{0}
minimizes the Hartree energy, and therefore

\displaystyle e_{\mathrm{H}}=\inf_{\|u\|=1}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{H}}[u]=u_{0}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot u_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\left(u_{0}\otimes u_{0}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot u_{0}\otimes u_{0}=A_{0}+B_{0},

where
A_{i}
and
B_{i}
are defined in Definition
3.4
. Since
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{H}}[u_{0}]\leq\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{H}}[u]
for
\|u\|=1
, we obtain by differentiation in any direction
z\perp u_{0}

\displaystyle 0=D|_{u_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{H}}(z)=u_{0}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot z+z^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot u_{0}+(z\otimes u_{0})^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot u_{0}\otimes u_{0}+(u_{0}\otimes u_{0})^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot z\otimes u_{0},

and consequently
u_{j}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot u_{0}+\left(u_{j}\otimes u_{0}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot u_{0}\otimes u_{0}=0
for all
j\geq 1
. Hence,

\displaystyle A_{1}+B_{1}=2\left(b_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot u_{0}+\left(b_{\geq 1}\ \underline{\otimes}\ u_{0}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot u_{0}\ \underline{\otimes}\ u_{0}\right)=0.

By Definition
4.3
and Lemma
4.1
, we have

\displaystyle N^{-1}\mathbb{H}=\mathfrak{Re}\left[A_{2}+B_{2}+B_{3}+B_{4}-\mu_{\mathrm{H}}\ b_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot b_{\geq 1}\right],

and consequently we can write for any
M\leq N
, using Eq. (
3.3
),

\displaystyle\pi_{M}\left(U_{N}N^{-1}\!H_{N}U_{N}^{-1}-e_{\mathrm{H}}-N^{-1}\mathbb{H}\right)\pi_{M}=\pi_{M}\mathfrak{Re}\left[X\right]\pi_{M}

with

\displaystyle X:=B_{0}\left(f_{0}\left(\mathbb{L}\right)\!-\!1\!+\!2\mathbb{L}\right)\!+\!B_{1}\left(f_{1}\left(\mathbb{L}\right)\!-\!\sqrt{1\!-\!\mathbb{L}}\right)\!+\!\sum_{r=2}^{4}B_{r}\left(f_{r}\left(\mathbb{L}\right)\!-\!1\right)\!+\!\sum_{r=5}^{6}B_{r}f_{r}\left(\mathbb{L}\right),

where we used
A_{1}\sqrt{1-\mathbb{L}}=-B_{1}\sqrt{1-\mathbb{L}}
. In order to estimate the first contribution, note that
|f_{0}(x)-1+2x|\leq 2\frac{M^{2}}{(N-1)N}
for all
0\leq x\leq\frac{M}{N}
and therefore

\displaystyle\pm\pi_{M}B_{0}\left(f_{0}\left(\mathbb{L}\right)-1+2\mathbb{L}\right)\pi_{M}=\pm\frac{1}{2}\hat{v}_{00,00}\pi_{M}\left(f_{0}\left(\mathbb{L}\right)-1+2\mathbb{L}\right)\pi_{M}\leq|\hat{v}_{00,00}|\frac{M^{2}}{(N-1)N}.

Recalling that
b_{\geq 1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}a_{\geq 1}
and Lemma
3.5
yields

\displaystyle\pm\pi_{M}B_{1}\left(f_{1}\left(\mathbb{L}\right)-\sqrt{1-\mathbb{L}}\right)\pi_{M}\leq c\frac{M}{N-1}\sqrt{\frac{M}{N}}.

Furthermore we obtain for
r\in\{2,3,4\}
by Lemma
3.5
with the choice
t=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}
, together with the bound
\sup_{x\leq\frac{M}{N}}|f_{r}(x)-1|\leq C\frac{M}{N}
for a constant
C>0
,

\displaystyle\pm\pi_{M}B_{r}\left(f_{r}\left(\mathbb{L}\right)-1\right)\pi_{M}\leq c\,C\frac{M^{\frac{3}{2}}}{N^{2}}\left(1+a_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot(T+1)\cdot a_{\geq 1}\right).

The estimates for
B_{5}f_{5}\left(\mathbb{L}\right)
and
B_{6}f_{6}\left(\mathbb{L}\right)
can be obtained analogously.
∎

Theorem 4.6

(Upper Bound)
.

Let
E_{N}
be the ground state energy of
H_{N}
,
e_{\mathrm{H}}
the Hartree energy defined in Eq. (
1.2
) and let
\mathbb{H}
be the Bogoliubov operator defined in Eq. (
4.2
). Given Assumption
1.1
, we have the upper bound

\displaystyle E_{N}\leq N\ e_{\mathrm{H}}+\inf\sigma\left(\mathbb{H}\right)+o_{N\rightarrow\infty}\left(1\right).

Proof.

Let
\nu
be the constant from Lemma
4.1
, such that the inequality
Q_{\mathrm{H}}+1\leq\nu\left(T|_{\mathcal{H}_{0}}+1\right)
holds. For all
\epsilon>0
, we know by Theorem
4.4
that there exists a state
\Psi\in\mathcal{F}_{M}
with
M<\infty
such that
\kappa:=\braket{a_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot(T+1)\cdot a_{\geq 1}}_{\Psi}<\infty
and
\braket{\mathbb{H}}_{\Psi}\leq\inf\sigma\left(\mathbb{H}\right)+\epsilon
. Applying Lemma
4.5
yields the estimate

\displaystyle\braket{H_{N}}_{U_{N}^{-1}\Psi}

\displaystyle\leq N\ e_{\mathrm{H}}+\braket{\mathbb{H}}_{\Psi}+C\ M\sqrt{\frac{M}{N}}\ \left(1+\braket{a_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot(T+1)\cdot a_{\geq 1}}_{\Psi}\right)

\displaystyle\leq N\ e_{\mathrm{H}}+\inf\sigma\left(\mathbb{H}\right)+\epsilon+C\ M\sqrt{\frac{M}{N}}\ \left(1+\kappa\right).

∎

4.3
Lower Bound

In the following, we will give the proof of the lower bound in the energy asymptotics in Eq. (
1.8
). First of all let us define the operators
q,p:\mathrm{dom}\left[\mathcal{N}\right]\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{0}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{0}
as

\displaystyle q:=\sum_{j=1}^{d}q_{j}\otimes u_{j}:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(b_{j}+b_{j}^{\dagger}\right)\otimes u_{j},

(4.4)

\displaystyle p:=\!\sum_{j=1}^{d}p_{j}\otimes u_{j}:=\frac{1}{2i}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(b_{j}-b_{j}^{\dagger}\right)\otimes u_{j},

(4.5)

which satisfy the commutation relations
[p_{k},q_{\ell}]=\frac{1}{2iN}\delta_{k,\ell}
. Recall that due to the translation-invariance of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{H}}
, the Hessian
\mathrm{Hess}|_{u_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{H}}
is degenerate on the real subspace
\{\sum_{j=1}^{d}t_{j}u_{j}:t_{j}\in\mathbb{\mathbb{R}}\}
. Therefore the Bogoliubov operator
\mathbb{H}
, which we have defined in Eq. (
4.2
) as the second quantization of the Hessian
\mathrm{Hess}|_{u_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{H}}
, is degenerate with respect to the operator
q
, i.e. it can be expressed only in terms of
p
,
b_{>d}
and
b_{>d}^{\dagger}
. Due to this degeneracy, we cannot directly apply the strategy pursued in
[
21
]
where the residuum of the Bogoliubov approximation is being estimated by the Bogoliubov operator itself. The problem is that the residuum
U_{N}H_{N}U_{N}^{-1}-Ne_{\mathrm{H}}-\mathbb{H}
includes contributions depending significantly on the modes
q_{j}
, like
q_{j}^{3}
, which we cannot compare with the Bogoliubov operator
\mathbb{H}
due to its degeneracy. Furthermore, it is insufficient to compare the residuum with the (rescaled) particle number operator
\frac{1}{N}\mathcal{N}
, which indeed dominates terms like
q_{j}^{3}
, since we only have the a priori information
\braket{\mathcal{N}}_{U_{N}\Psi_{N}}=o(N)
provided by Bose–Einstein condensation. The novel idea of this Subsection and the subsequent Section
5
is to apply a further unitary transformation
\mathcal{W}_{N}
such that the residuum
\mathcal{W}_{N}U_{N}H_{N}U_{N}^{-1}\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}-Ne_{\mathrm{H}}-\mathbb{H}
no longer includes this kind of contributions and consequently we can compare the residuum with the Bogoliubov operator
\mathbb{H}
. This leads to the important inequality in Eq. (
1.13
). As a consequence we observe that, in contrast to the particle number operator
\mathcal{N}
, the Bogoliubov operator satisfies
\braket{\mathbb{H}}_{U_{N}\Psi_{N}}=O(1)
, which, a posteriori, justifies estimating the residuum by the Bogoliubov operator.

Before we are going to construct a unitary map
\mathcal{W}_{N}
satisfying Eq. (
1.13
), we are solving the corresponding problem on a classical level, i.e. we are going to construct a map
F
which satisfies Eq. (
1.11
). We will then define
\mathcal{W}_{N}
as the quantum counterpart to
F
.

Definition 4.7
.

For any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let us recall the functions
u_{0,y}(x):=u_{0}(x-y)
defined in Assumption
1.1
and let us define the map
\lambda:\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\lambda(y):=\left(u_{j}^{\dagger}\cdot u_{0,y}\right)_{j=1}^{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Note that
u_{j}
and
u_{0,y}
are real-valued functions, and therefore
\lambda
is indeed
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued. Since
y\mapsto u_{0,y}
is a
C^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{H}\right)
function by Assumption
1.3
,
D_{y}\lambda(0)
has full rank and
\lambda(0)=0
, there exists a local inverse
\lambda^{-1}:B_{2\delta}(0)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
\delta>0
small enough, where
B_{r}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the ball of radius
r
centered around the origin. Let
0\leq\sigma\leq 1
be a smooth function with
\sigma|_{B_{\delta}(0)}=1
and
\mathrm{supp}(\sigma)\subset B_{2\delta}(0)
. Then we define the function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle f(t):

\displaystyle=\sigma(t)\left[u_{0,\lambda^{-1}(t)}-\left(u_{0}^{\dagger}\cdot u_{0,\lambda^{-1}(t)}\right)u_{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{d}t_{j}\ u_{j}\right]=\sum_{j=d+1}^{\infty}f_{j}(t)u_{j},

(4.6)

with
f_{j}(t):=\sigma(t)u_{j}^{\dagger}\cdot u_{0,\lambda^{-1}(t)}
. Note that
t\mapsto f(t)
is a
C^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{H}_{0}\right)
function, due to the regularity of
y\mapsto u_{0,y}
. Furthermore,
f(0)=0
. We can now define the map
F:\mathcal{H}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{0}
for all
z=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(t_{j}+is_{j}\right)u_{j}+z_{>d}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
with
t,s\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
z_{>d}\in\{u_{1},\dots,u_{d}\}^{\perp}
as

\displaystyle F\left(z\right):=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(t_{j}+is^{\prime}_{j}\right)u_{j}+z_{>d}+f(t),

(4.7)

where
s^{\prime}_{j}:=s_{j}-\mathfrak{Im}\left[\partial_{j}f(t)^{\dagger}\cdot z_{>d}\right]
.

The essential property of
F
is that
\iota\circ F
, where
\iota
is the embedding defined in Eq. (
1.10
), maps the set
\{\sum_{j=1}^{d}t_{j}u_{j}:|t|<\delta\}
into the set of Hartree minimizers

\displaystyle\iota\circ F\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}t_{j}u_{j}\right)=\iota\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}t_{j}u_{j}+f(t)\right)=u_{0,\lambda^{-1}(t)},

for all
|t|<\delta
. This also implies the central inequality Eq. (
1.11
), as will be demonstrated in the introduction of Section
5
.

The arguments so far are based only on the fact that
F
shifts the component
z_{>d}
by an amount
f(t)
. The identity
(\iota\circ F)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}t_{j}u_{j}\right)=u_{0,\lambda^{-1}(t)}
would still hold if we used
s_{j}
instead of
s_{j}^{\prime}
in Eq. (
4.7
). Nevertheless, it is natural that
F
shifts the
s
component as well, since this shift makes sure that
\mathrm{d}F
preserves the symplectic form
\omega(z_{1},z_{2}):=\mathfrak{Re}\left[z_{1}\right]^{\dagger}\cdot\mathfrak{Im}\left[z_{2}\right]-\mathfrak{Im}\left[z_{1}\right]^{\dagger}\cdot\mathfrak{Re}\left[z_{2}\right]
. Therefore it makes sense to look for a quantum counterpart
\mathcal{W}_{N}
, which we are going to define in the subsequent Definition
4.8
. In analogy to
F
preserving the symplectic form
\omega
, the unitary map
\mathcal{W}_{N}
is preserving the commutator bracket.

Definition 4.8

(Unitary Transformation
\mathcal{W}_{N}:\mathcal{F}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{0}
)
.

Based on the fact that the operators
q_{1},\dots,q_{d}
defined in Eq. (
4.4
) commute, we can assign to a function
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{0}
with components
h_{j}(t):=u_{j}^{\dagger}\cdot h(t)
an operator
h(q):\mathcal{F}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{0}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{0}

\displaystyle h(q):=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}h_{j}(q_{1},\dots,q_{d})\otimes u_{j},

where the operators
h_{j}(q_{1},\dots,q_{d})
are well defined via functional calculus. Let
f
be the function defined in Eq. (
4.6
), then we can define the unitary map
\mathcal{W}_{N}:\mathcal{F}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{0}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{N}:=\mathrm{exp}\left[Nf(q)^{\dagger}\cdot b_{>d}-Nb_{>d}^{\dagger}\cdot f(q)\right]=\mathrm{exp}\left[N\sum_{j=d+1}^{\infty}f_{j}(q_{1},\dots,q_{d})\left(b_{j}-b_{j}^{\dagger}\right)\right],

(4.8)

where we have used that
u_{j}^{\dagger}\cdot f(t)=0
for
j\in\{1,\dots,d\}
. Note that
q_{1},\dots,q_{d}
and
b_{>d}
have an
N
dependence, which we suppress in our notation. Furthermore, we define the transformed operators

\displaystyle p^{\prime}_{j}:

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}_{N}\,p_{j}\,\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1},

\displaystyle p^{\prime}:

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}_{N}\,p\,\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}p^{\prime}_{j}\otimes u_{j},

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{\prime}:

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}_{N}\,\mathbb{L}\,\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1},

where
p
is defined in Eq. (
4.5
) and
\mathbb{L}
is defined in Definition
3.1
. Note that the domain of
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{W}_{N}\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}
, since
\mathbb{L}
is only defined on
\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}
.

That the unitary map
\mathcal{W}_{N}
is indeed a quantum counterpart to the classical map
F
defined in Eq. (
4.7
) can be seen from the transformation laws described in the following Lemma
4.9
.

Lemma 4.9

(Transformation Laws)
.

We have the following transformation laws

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{N}\,b_{j}\,\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}=b_{j}+f_{j}(q)\mbox{ for }j>d,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{N}\,q_{j}\,\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}=q_{j}\mbox{ for }j\in\{1,\dots,d\},

\displaystyle p^{\prime}_{j}=p_{j}-\mathfrak{Im}\left[\partial_{u_{j}}f(q)^{\dagger}\cdot b_{>d}\right]\mbox{ for }j\in\{1,\dots,d\},

and therefore
\mathcal{W}_{N}\,b_{\geq 1}\,\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}=q+ip^{\prime}+b_{>d}+f(q)
.

The proof of Lemma
4.9
is elementary and is left to the reader. Before we state the main Theorems of this subsection, let us define what it means for a sequence of operators
X_{N}
to be asymptotically small compared to another sequence
Y_{N}
, in a suitable sense that is specific to our problem.

Definition 4.10
.

We say that sequences of operators
X_{N},Y_{N}
with
Y_{N}\geq 0
satisfy

\displaystyle X_{N}=o_{*}(Y_{N}),

in case for all
\epsilon>0
there exists a
\delta>0
, such that
\big|\braket{X_{N}}_{\Psi}\big|\leq\epsilon\ \braket{Y_{N}}_{\Psi}
for all
M,N
with
\frac{M}{N}\leq\delta
and all elements
\Psi\in\mathcal{W}_{N}\mathcal{F}_{\leq M}
. Furthermore, we say that sequences of operators
X_{N},Y_{N}
with
Y_{N}\geq 0
satisfy

\displaystyle X_{N}=O_{*}(Y_{N}),

in case there exists a constant
C
and
\delta_{0}>0
, such that
\big|\braket{X_{N}}_{\Psi}\big|\leq C\ \braket{Y_{N}}_{\Psi}
for all
M,N
with
\frac{M}{N}\leq\delta_{0}
and all
\Psi\in\mathcal{W}_{N}\mathcal{F}_{\leq M}
.

Remark 4.11
.

Let us denote with
\pi_{M,N}:=\mathcal{W}_{N}\,\pi_{M}\,\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}
the orthogonal projection onto the subspace
\mathcal{W}_{N}\mathcal{F}_{\leq M}\subset\mathcal{F}_{0}
. Then the statement
X_{N}=O_{*}\left(Y_{N}\right)
holds true if and only if there exists a constant
C
and
\delta_{0}>0
, such that

\displaystyle\pi_{M,N}\,\mathfrak{Re}\left[\lambda X_{N}\right]\pi_{M,N}\leq C\ \pi_{M,N}Y_{N}\pi_{M,N}

(4.9)

for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\lambda|=1
and
\frac{M}{N}\leq\delta_{0}
. Similarly,
X_{N}=o_{*}\left(Y_{N}\right)
is equivalent to the existence of a function
\epsilon:\mathbb{R}^{+}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
with
\underset{\delta\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\ \epsilon(\delta)=0
, such that

\displaystyle\pi_{M,N}\,\mathfrak{Re}\left[\lambda X_{N}\right]\pi_{M,N}\leq\epsilon\left(\frac{M}{N}\right)\pi_{M,N}Y_{N}\pi_{M,N}

(4.10)

for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\lambda|=1
and
M\leq N
.

Theorem 4.12
.

Recall the
o_{*}(\cdot)
notation from Definition
4.10
, the Hartree energy
e_{\mathrm{H}}
defined in Eq. (
1.2
) and the Bogoliubov operator
\mathbb{H}
defined in Eq. (
4.2
), and let us define

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{N}:=p^{\dagger}\cdot p+b_{>d}^{\dagger}\cdot(T+1)\cdot b_{>d}+\frac{1}{N}.

(4.11)

Then, given Assumptions
1.1
and
1.3
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{W}_{N}U_{N}\right)N^{-1}\!H_{N}\left(\mathcal{W}_{N}U_{N}\right)^{-1}=e_{\mathrm{H}}+N^{-1}\mathbb{H}+o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right).

The proof of Theorem
4.12
, which in particular gives rise to a rigorous version of the key inequality Eq. (
1.13
), will be the content of Section
5
. With Theorem
4.12
at hand we can verify the lower bound in the main Theorem
1.4
.

Theorem 4.13

(Lower Bound)
.

Let
E_{N}
be the ground state energy of
H_{N}
,
e_{\mathrm{H}}
the Hartree energy defined in Eq. (
1.2
) and let
\mathbb{H}
be the Bogoliubov operator defined in Eq. (
4.2
). Given Assumptions
1.1
and
1.3
, we have the lower bound

\displaystyle E_{N}\geq N\,e_{\mathrm{H}}+\inf\sigma\left(\mathbb{H}\right)+o_{N\rightarrow\infty}\left(1\right).

Proof.

According to Theorem
1.2
, there exists a sequence of states
\Psi_{N}\in\bigotimes_{\mathrm{s}}^{N}\mathcal{H}
,
\|\Psi_{N}\|=1
, such that
\braket{H_{N}}_{\Psi_{N}}\leq E_{N}+\alpha_{N}
with
\alpha_{N}\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
and

\displaystyle\epsilon_{N}:=\braket{b_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot b_{\geq 1}}_{U_{N}\Psi_{N}}=\braket{b_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot b_{\geq 1}}_{\Psi_{N}}\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

Let us abbreviate
\widetilde{H}_{N}:=U_{N}H_{N}U_{N}^{-1}
and let
\pi_{M}
be the orthogonal projection onto the space
\mathcal{F}_{\leq M}
as before. Furthermore, let
0\leq f,g\leq 1
be smooth functions with
f^{2}+g^{2}=1
,
f(x)=1
for
x\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
f(x)=0
for
x\geq 1
, and let us define
f_{M}(x):=f\left(\frac{x}{M}\right)
and
g_{M}(x):=g\left(\frac{x}{M}\right)
. Then the generalized IMS localization formula in
[
23
, Theorem A.1]
, in the form stated in
[
21
, Proposition 6.1]
, tells us that

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}_{N}=f_{M}\left(\mathcal{N}\right)\,\widetilde{H}_{N}\,f_{M}\left(\mathcal{N}\right)+g_{M}\left(\mathcal{N}\right)\,\widetilde{H}_{N}\,g_{M}\left(\mathcal{N}\right)-R_{M,N},

with
R_{M,N}\leq\frac{R}{M^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}\left(\widetilde{H}_{N}-E_{N}\right)P_{n}
, where
P_{n}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{F}_{\leq n}\cap\mathcal{F}_{\leq n-1}^{\perp}
,
\mathcal{N}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}^{\dagger}a_{j}
and
R:=16\left(\|f^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\infty}+\left\|g^{\prime}\right\|^{2}_{\infty}\right)
. Let us define
M_{N}
as the smallest integer larger than
\sqrt{\epsilon_{N}}N
and
N^{\frac{2}{3}}
. The exponent
\frac{2}{3}
is somewhat arbitrary and we could use any sequence
\ell_{N}
with
N^{\frac{1}{2}}\ll\ell_{N}\ll N
instead. Using the estimate
1-f_{M}(x)^{2}\leq\frac{2}{M}x
yields

\displaystyle\rho_{N}:=\braket{1\!-\!f_{M_{N}}(\mathcal{N})^{2}}_{U_{N}\Psi_{N}}\leq\frac{2}{M_{N}}\braket{\mathcal{N}}_{U_{N}\Psi_{N}}=\frac{2N}{M_{N}}\braket{b_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot b_{\geq 1}}_{U_{N}\Psi_{N}}\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\epsilon_{N}}}\epsilon_{N}\!\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}\!0.

Let us define
\Phi_{N}:=(1-\rho_{N})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ f_{M_{N}}(\mathcal{N})U_{N}\Psi_{N}
. Using Lemma
3.6
and the inequality
\widetilde{H}_{N}\geq E_{N}
yields

\displaystyle E_{N}\!+\!\alpha_{N}\!\geq\!\braket{\widetilde{H}_{N}}_{U_{N}\Psi_{N}}\!\geq\!(1\!-\!\rho_{N})\braket{\widetilde{H}_{N}}_{\Phi_{N}}\!+\!\rho_{N}E_{N}\!-\!\frac{R}{M_{N}^{2}}\braket{k\widetilde{H}_{N}\!+\!k^{2}N\!-\!E_{N}}_{U_{N}\Psi_{N}}.

(4.12)

Since
\lim_{N}N^{-1}E_{N}=e_{\mathrm{H}}
, we obtain that
\beta_{N}:=\frac{R}{M_{N}^{2}}\braket{k\widetilde{H}_{N}+k^{2}N-E_{N}}_{U_{N}\Psi_{N}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\beta_{N}\leq\frac{R}{N^{\frac{4}{3}}}\left((k-1)E_{N}+k\alpha_{N}+k^{2}N\right)\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0.

We can now rewrite Inequality (
4.12
) as

\displaystyle E_{N}\geq\braket{\widetilde{H}_{N}}_{\Phi_{N}}-\frac{\alpha_{N}+\beta_{N}}{1-\rho_{N}}.

Let
r>0
be as in the assumption of Theorem
4.4
and recall the definition of
\mathbb{A}
in Eq. (
4.3
). Note that
N\mathbb{T}_{N}=\mathbb{A}+1
. By Theorem
4.12
and Remark
4.11
, there exists a function
\epsilon
with
\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\epsilon(\delta)
, such that

\displaystyle\braket{\widetilde{H}_{N}}_{\Phi_{N}}\geq N\ e_{\mathrm{H}}+\braket{\mathbb{H}}_{\mathcal{W}_{N}\Phi_{N}}-\epsilon\left(\frac{M_{N}}{N}\right)\ \braket{\mathbb{A}+1}_{\mathcal{W}_{N}\Phi_{N}}

\displaystyle\ \ =N\ e_{\mathrm{H}}+\left(1-\frac{1}{r}\epsilon\left(\frac{M_{N}}{N}\right)\right)\braket{\mathbb{H}}_{\mathcal{W}_{N}\Phi_{N}}+\frac{1}{r}\epsilon\left(\frac{M_{N}}{N}\right)\braket{\mathbb{H}-r\mathbb{A}}_{\mathcal{W}_{N}\Phi_{N}}-\epsilon\left(\frac{M_{N}}{N}\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \geq N\ e_{\mathrm{H}}+\inf\sigma\left(\mathbb{H}\right)+\frac{1}{r}\epsilon\left(\frac{M_{N}}{N}\right)\big(\inf\sigma\left(\mathbb{H}-r\mathbb{A}\right)-\inf\sigma\left(\mathbb{H}\right)\big)-\epsilon\left(\frac{M_{N}}{N}\right)

for all
N
large enough such that
1-\frac{1}{r}\epsilon\left(\frac{M_{N}}{N}\right)\geq 0
. This concludes the proof, since
\inf\sigma\left(\mathbb{H}-r\mathbb{A}\right)>-\infty
by Theorem
4.4
.
∎

5
Taylor Expansion of
\left(\mathcal{W}_{N}U_{N}\right)H_{N}\left(\mathcal{W}_{N}U_{N}\right)^{-1}

This section is devoted to the verification of the main technical Theorem
4.12
, which is the rigorous version of inequality Eq. (
1.13
). Before we explain the proof, recall the definition of
\iota
in Eq. (
1.10
) and
F
in Eq. (
4.7
), and let us verify the classical counterpart Eq. (
1.11
). For this purpose we define the functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(z):=\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{H}}\left[\iota\left(F(z)\right)\right],

(5.1)

which satisfies according to the definition of
F
that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\left(\vec{t}\right)=e_{\mathrm{H}}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\vec{t}:=\sum_{j=1}^{d}t_{j}u_{j}
, i.e.
F
flattens the manifold of minimizers of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{H}}\circ\iota
. We will verify Eq. (
1.11
) by sorting the functional
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
in terms of powers in the variables
s
and
z_{>d}
for any
z=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(t_{j}+is_{j}\right)u_{j}+z_{>d}\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
with
z_{>d}\in\{u_{1},\dots,u_{d}\}^{\perp}
. In the following, let
\pi(z):=\sum_{j=1}^{d}is_{j}u_{j}+z_{>d}
be the projection onto
\mathcal{V}:=\pi\left(\mathcal{H}_{0}\right)
. We can now sort
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(z)
in terms of powers in
s
and
z_{>d}
, i.e. in terms of powers in
\pi(z)
, using a Taylor approximation with expansion point
\vec{t}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\left(\vec{t}+\pi(z)\right)=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)\!+D|_{\vec{t}}\ \mathcal{E}^{\prime}\big(\pi(z)\big)+\frac{1}{2}D^{2}|_{\vec{t}}\ \mathcal{E}^{\prime}\big(\pi(z)\big)+\{\ \mathrm{HigherOrders}\ \}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)\!+D_{\mathcal{V}}|_{\vec{t}}\ \mathcal{E}^{\prime}(z)+\frac{1}{2}D_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}|_{\vec{t}}\ \mathcal{E}^{\prime}\big(z\big)+\{\ \mathrm{HigherOrders}\ \},

(5.2)

where
D|_{z_{0}}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(v)
is the first derivative of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
in the direction
v
at
z_{0}
,
D^{2}|_{z_{0}}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(v)
is the second derivative in the direction
v
, and
D_{\mathcal{V}}|_{z_{0}}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(v):=D|_{z_{0}}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\left(\pi(v)\right)
and
D_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}|_{z_{0}}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\big(v\big):=D^{2}|_{z_{0}}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\big(\pi(v)\big)
are the derivatives only with respect to directions in
\mathcal{V}
. Using
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)=e_{\mathrm{H}}
,
D_{\mathcal{V}}|_{\vec{t}}\ \mathcal{E}^{\prime}=0
and the fact that
D_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}|_{\vec{t}}\,\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(v)\geq\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\right)D_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}|_{0}\ \mathcal{E}^{\prime}(v)
for
t
small enough by continuity, we formally arrive at Eq. (
1.11
), which is claimed to hold only for small
\|z\|^{2}=|t|^{2}+\|\pi(z)\|^{2}
anyway.

By sorting the expression
\left(\mathcal{W}_{N}U_{N}\right)H_{N}\left(\mathcal{W}_{N}U_{N}\right)^{-1}
in terms of powers in the operators
p
and
b_{>d}
, we will verify that we end up with the same Taylor approximation we obtained by sorting
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(z)
in terms of powers in the variables
s
and
z_{>d}
. More precisely, our goal is to verify that

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{W}_{N}U_{N}\right)\!N^{-1}\!H_{N}\left(\mathcal{W}_{N}U_{N}\right)^{-1}\!

\displaystyle=\!\mathcal{E}\!\left(q\right)\!+\!D_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{q}\mathcal{E}\Big(b_{\geq 1}\Big)\!+\!\frac{1}{2}D_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\big|_{0}\mathcal{E}\Big(b_{\geq 1}\Big)\!+\!o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)

(5.3)

\displaystyle=e_{\mathrm{H}}+N^{-1}\mathbb{H}+o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right),

where
\mathcal{E}:\mathrm{dom}\left[T\right]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a differentiable extension to all of
\mathrm{dom}\left[T\right]
of the functional
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\big|_{B_{r}}
, restricted to the ball
B_{r}:=\{z\in\mathcal{H}_{0}\cap\mathrm{dom}\left[T\right]:\|z\|<r\}
for a sufficiently small
r>0
. Note that the spectrum of the operators
q_{1},\dots,q_{d}
is the whole real axis
\mathbb{R}
. In order to even define
\mathcal{E}\left(q\right)
and
D_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{q}\mathcal{E}\Big(b_{\geq 1}\Big)
with the help of functional calculus, it is therefore necessary that
\mathcal{E}
, in contrast to
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
, is an everywhere defined and differentiable functional. For such a function
\mathcal{E}
we can define
\mathcal{E}\left(q\right)
via functional calculus starting from the function
t\mapsto\mathcal{E}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}t_{j}u_{j}\right)
for
t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
The so far formal objects
D_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{q}\mathcal{E}\Big(b_{\geq 1}\Big)
and
\frac{1}{2}D_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\big|_{0}\mathcal{E}\Big(b_{\geq 1}\Big)
are later defined in Definition
5.4
. Note that it is a necessity to restrict
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
to a sufficiently small ball
B_{r}
first, to be precise we require that
\|F(z)\|\leq 1-\delta
for all
z\in B_{r}
where
0<\delta<1
, since
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
itself does not have a differentiable extension due to the square root appearing in the definition of
\iota
, see Eq. (
1.10
).

In order to reduce the technical efforts of proving Eq. (
5.3
), we will make use of the fact that

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{W}_{N}U_{N}\right)N^{-1}H_{N}\left(\mathcal{W}_{N}U_{N}\right)^{-1}=\mathcal{W}_{N}\widetilde{A}_{N}\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}+\mathcal{W}_{N}\widetilde{B}_{N}\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}+o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right),

which, as we will see in the proof of Theorem
4.12
, is a consequence of Eq. (
3.8
). We can then prove Eq. (
5.3
) separately for the operators
\mathcal{W}_{N}\,\widetilde{A}_{N}\,\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{W}_{N}\,\widetilde{B}_{N}\,\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}
. In fact, we are going to verify that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{N}\,\widetilde{A}_{N}\,\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{A}\left(q\right)\!+D_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{q}\mathcal{E}_{A}\Big(b_{\geq 1}\Big)+\!\frac{1}{2}D_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\big|_{0}\mathcal{E}_{A}\Big(b_{\geq 1}\Big)+\frac{c}{N}+o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right),

(5.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{N}\,\widetilde{B}_{N}\,\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{B}\left(q\right)\!+D_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{q}\mathcal{E}_{B}\Big(b_{\geq 1}\Big)+\!\frac{1}{2}D_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\big|_{0}\mathcal{E}_{B}\Big(b_{\geq 1}\Big)-\frac{c}{N}+o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right),

(5.5)

where the constant
c
arises due to the non-commutative nature of the operators
q
and
p
, and
\mathcal{E}_{A}
and
\mathcal{E}_{B}
are differentiable extensions of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{A},\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{B}:B_{r}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{A}(z):

\displaystyle=u_{z}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot u_{z},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{B}(z):=\frac{1}{2}\left(u_{z}\otimes u_{z}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot u_{z}\otimes u_{z},

(5.6)

where
u_{z}:=\iota\left(F(z)\right)
. The proofs of Eqs. (
5.4
) and (
5.5
) will be carried out in Subsections
5.1
and
5.2
, respectively. We have to perform a variety of operator estimates, and since
\mathcal{W}_{N}\widetilde{A}_{N}\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{W}_{N}\widetilde{B}_{N}\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}
involve factors of the form
\sqrt{1-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}}
with
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
defined in Definition
4.8
, we need in particular to estimate the Taylor residuum corresponding to approximations of such terms. The operator estimates can be found in Appendix
C
, respectively Appendix
D
for the operator square root specifically.

5.1
Taylor Expansion of
\mathcal{W}_{N}\widetilde{A}_{N}\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}

In order to structure the analysis, we split the operator
\mathcal{W}_{N}\widetilde{A}_{N}\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}
into simpler operators
H_{J}
, introduced in Definition
5.1
, and we split the classical counterpart
\mathcal{E}_{A}
defined in Eq. (
5.6
) into atoms
\mathcal{E}_{J}
, defined in Definition
5.2
. In Lemma
5.3
, we then explain how
\mathcal{W}_{N}\widetilde{A}_{N}\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{A}
can be written in terms of
H_{J}
and
\mathcal{E}_{J}
, respectively.

Definition 5.1
.

Recall the function
t\mapsto f(t)
from Definition
4.7
. For
i\in\{0,\dots,4\}
, we define operators
h_{i}:\mathrm{dom}[\mathcal{N}]\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{0}\otimes\mathcal{H}
by
h_{0}:=1_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}\otimes u_{0}
and

\displaystyle h_{1}:

\displaystyle=q=\sum_{j=1}^{d}q_{j}\otimes u_{j},\ \ \ \ \ h_{3}:=ip^{\prime}=i\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(p_{j}-\mathfrak{Im}\left[\partial_{j}f(q)^{\dagger}\cdot b_{>d}\right]\right)\otimes u_{j},

\displaystyle h_{2}:

\displaystyle=f(q),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ h_{4}:=b_{>d},

where
f(q)
and
\partial_{j}f(q)
are defined according to Definition
4.8
. Furthermore, for a multi-index
J=(i,j)
with
i,j\in\{0,\dots,4\}
we define an operator
H_{J}
on
\mathcal{W}_{N}\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}
as

\displaystyle H_{J}:

\displaystyle=h_{i}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot h_{j}\,\Big(1-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\Big)^{\frac{m_{J}}{2}},

where
m_{J}
counts how many of the indices in
J=(i,j)
are zero.

Definition 5.2
.

Let us decompose an arbitrary
z\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
as
z=\sum_{j=1}^{d}(t_{j}+is_{j})\ u_{j}+z_{>d}
, with
t,s\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
z_{>d}\in\{u_{1},\dots,u_{d}\}^{\perp}
. For
i\in\{0,\dots,4\}
, we define in analogy to Definition
5.1
the functions
e_{i}:\mathcal{H}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}
by
e_{0}(z):=u_{0}
and

\displaystyle e_{1}(z):

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}t_{j}\ u_{j},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ e_{3}(z):=i\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(s_{j}-\mathfrak{Im}\left[\partial_{j}f(t_{1},\dots,t_{d})^{\dagger}\cdot z\right]\right)u_{j},

\displaystyle e_{2}(z):

\displaystyle=f\left(t\right),\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ e_{4}(z):=z_{>d}.

With this at hand, we can write the transformation
F:\mathcal{H}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{0}
from Eq. (
4.7
) as

\displaystyle F(z)=e_{1}(z)+e_{2}(z)+e_{3}(z)+e_{4}(z).

Furthermore, consider for
m\in\{0,\dots,4\}
the functions

\displaystyle\eta_{m}\left(z\right):=\begin{cases}\Big(1-\|F(z)\|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{m}{2}}\hbox{ for even $m$},\\
\chi\big(\|F(z)\|^{2}\big)\Big(1-\|F(z)\|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{m}{2}}\hbox{ for odd $m$},\end{cases}

(5.7)

where
\chi
is a smooth function with
0\leq\chi(x)\leq 1
,
\mathrm{supp}\left(\chi\right)\subset[0,1)
and
\chi(x)=1
for
|x|<\frac{1}{2}
. Then we can define for a multi-index
J=(i,j)
with
i,j\in\{0,\dots,4\}
the function
\mathcal{E}_{J}:\mathcal{H}_{0}\cap\mathrm{dom}[T]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{J}(z):=e_{i}(z)^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot e_{j}(z)\ \eta_{m_{J}}\left(z\right),

where
m_{J}
counts how many of the two indices
i,j
are zero.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let us define for all
i,j\in\{1,\dots,4\}
the coefficients
\lambda_{(0,0)}:=1
,
\lambda_{(i,0)}:=2
,
\lambda_{(i,j)}:=1
and
\lambda_{(0,j)}:=0
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{N}\,\widetilde{A}_{N}\,\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\in\{0,\dots,4\}^{2}}\lambda_{J}\ \mathfrak{Re}\left[H_{J}\right],

(5.8)

where
\widetilde{A}_{N}
is defined in Eq. (
3.6
). Furthermore, the functional
\mathcal{E}_{A}
defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{A}(z):

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\in\{0,\dots,4\}^{2}}\lambda_{J}\ \mathfrak{Re}\left[\mathcal{E}_{J}(z)\right],

(5.9)

is an extension of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{A}\big|_{B_{r}}
defined in Eq. (
5.6
), where
B_{r}:=\{z\in\mathcal{H}_{0}\cap\mathrm{dom}\left[T\right]:\|z\|<r\}
and
r>0
is a constant such that
\|F(z)\|<\frac{1}{2}
for all
z\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
with
\|z\|<r
.

Note that the operator
\mathcal{W}_{N}\,\widetilde{A}_{N}\,\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}
involves terms with
\sqrt{1-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}}
on the right side as well as on the left side. In order to reduce the technical effort later, it will be convenient to have all of them on one side, say the right side. This can be achieved by using the real part, e.g. we can write for
j\in\{1,\dots,4\}

\displaystyle h_{j}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot u_{0}\sqrt{1-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}}+\sqrt{1-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}}u_{0}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot h_{j}=\mathfrak{Re}\left[h_{j}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot u_{0}\sqrt{1-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}}\right]=2\mathfrak{Re}\left[H_{(j,0)}\right].

Therefore we set all the coefficients
\lambda_{(0,j)}
in Lemma
5.3
to zero, since the
(0,j)
-contribution is already included in the real part of the
(j,0)
-contribution.

Proof.

Eq. (
5.8
) follows from the transformation law
\mathcal{W}_{N}\,b_{\geq 1}\,\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}=h_{1}+h_{2}+h_{3}+h_{4}
, where
h_{i}
is defined in Definition
5.1
, and the definition
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}=\mathcal{W}_{N}\,\mathbb{L}\,\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}
. Similarly we obtain
\mathcal{E}_{A}(z)=\mathcal{E}_{A}^{\prime}(z)
for all
z
with
\|z\|<r
for
r
as above and the fact that

\displaystyle\iota\big(F(z)\big)=\iota\left(e_{1}(z)\!+\!e_{2}(z)\!+\!e_{3}(z)\!+\!e_{4}(z)\right)=\sqrt{1\!-\!\|F(z)\|^{2}}e_{0}\!+\!e_{1}(z)\!+\!e_{2}(z)\!+\!e_{3}(z)\!+\!e_{4}(z).

∎

In order to prove the Taylor approximation in Eq. (
5.4
), we will verify that each of the atoms
H_{J}
can be approximated using the quantized Taylor coefficients of
\mathcal{E}_{J}
. The quantized Taylor coefficients
D_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{q}\mathcal{E}_{J}\Big(b_{\geq 1}\Big)
and
D_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}\big|_{0}\ \mathcal{E}_{J}\Big(b_{\geq 1}\Big)
are rigorously defined by the following Definition.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
L_{t}:\mathcal{H}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a bounded
\mathbb{R}
-linear map for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and let
w(t),\widetilde{w}(t)
be the unique elements in
\mathcal{H}_{0}
such that
L_{t}(z)=w(t)^{\dagger}\cdot z+z^{\dagger}\cdot\widetilde{w}(t)
. Then we define

\displaystyle L_{q}\left(b_{\geq 1}\right):=w(q)^{\dagger}\cdot b_{\geq 1}+b_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot\widetilde{w}(q).

(5.10)

Let furthermore
\Lambda
be an
\mathbb{R}
-quadratic form on
\mathcal{H}_{0}
with a unique decomposition
\Lambda(z)=z^{\dagger}\cdot Q\cdot z+G^{\dagger}\cdot z\otimes z+\left(z\otimes z\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\widetilde{G}
where
Q
is an operator on
\mathcal{H}_{0}
and
G,\widetilde{G}\in\mathcal{H}\otimes_{\mathrm{s}}\mathcal{H}_{0}
(or, more generally, in
\overline{\mathcal{H}_{0}\otimes_{\mathrm{s}}\mathcal{H}_{0}}^{\|.\|_{*}}
as introduced in Lemma
4.1
). Then we define
\Lambda\left(b_{\geq 1}\right)
as

\displaystyle\Lambda\left(b_{\geq 1}\right):=b_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot Q\cdot b_{\geq 1}+G^{\dagger}\cdot b_{\geq 1}\ \underline{\otimes}\ b_{\geq 1}+\left(b_{\geq 1}\ \underline{\otimes}\ b_{\geq 1}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\widetilde{G}.

In the following we want to verify that the residuum
R_{J}
defined as

\displaystyle R_{J}:

\displaystyle=H_{J}-\mathcal{E}_{J}(q)-D_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{q}\mathcal{E}_{J}\left(b_{\geq 1}\right)-\frac{1}{2}D^{2}_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{0}\mathcal{E}_{J}\left(b_{\geq 1}\right)-\frac{c_{J}}{N}

(5.11)

is small, where the constant
c_{J}
are given by

\displaystyle c_{(0,0)}:

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{4}\ u_{0}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot u_{0}=-\frac{1}{8}\ \sum_{j=1}^{d}\partial_{t_{j}}^{2}\big|_{t=0}\mathcal{E}_{(0,0)}\left(\vec{t}\ \right),

\displaystyle c_{(3,3)}:

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\ \sum_{j=1}^{d}u_{j}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot u_{j}=\frac{1}{8}\ \sum_{j=1}^{d}\partial_{t_{j}}^{2}\big|_{t=0}\mathcal{E}_{(1,1)}\left(\vec{t}\ \right),

(5.12)

c_{(1,3)}:=c_{(3,1)}:=-c_{(3,3)}
and
c_{J}:=0
for all other
J\in\{0,\dots,4\}^{2}
, where
\vec{t\,}:=\sum_{j=1}^{d}t_{j}u_{j}
. The proof will be spit into two parts. In Lemma
5.6
we derive an explicit representation of the residuum
R_{J}
by sorting the operator
H_{J}
in terms of powers in
p
and
b_{>d}
, and in Theorem
5.7
we will make sure that this residuum is indeed small compared to the operator
\mathbb{T}_{N}
defined in Eq. (
4.11
), which is quadratic in the operators
p
and
b_{>d}
.

In order to illustrate the emergence of the additional constants
c_{J}
in the residuum
R_{J}
in Eq. (
5.11
), let us first investigate the following toy problem.

Example
. Consider the toy Hamiltonian
H_{\mathrm{toy}}:=b_{1}^{\dagger}b_{1}
and the corresponding Hartree functional
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{toy}}:\mathbb{C}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
given by
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{toy}}[z]:=|z|^{2}
. Using
b_{1}=q_{1}+ip_{1}
and the commutation relation
[ip_{1},q_{1}]=\frac{1}{2N}
, we obtain

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{toy}}=q_{1}^{2}\!+\!p_{1}^{2}\!-\!\frac{1}{2N}=q_{1}^{2}\!-\!\frac{1}{4}\left(b_{1}\!-\!b_{1}^{\dagger}\right)^{2}\!-\!\frac{1}{2N}=q_{1}^{2}\!+\!\frac{1}{2}b_{1}^{\dagger}b_{1}\!-\!\frac{1}{4}b_{1}^{2}\!-\!\frac{1}{4}\left(b_{1}^{\dagger}\right)^{2}\!-\!\frac{1}{4N}.

(5.13)

Let
D_{\mathcal{V}}
be the derivative with respect to the imaginary part and
z=t+is\in\mathbb{C}
, then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}D^{2}_{\mathcal{V}}|_{0}\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{toy}}(z)=\frac{1}{2}D^{2}|_{0}\,\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{toy}}(is)=s^{2}=\frac{1}{2}|z|^{2}-\frac{1}{4}z^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\bar{z}^{2}.

With the definition
c_{\mathrm{toy}}:=-\frac{1}{8}\partial_{t}^{2}\big|_{t=0}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{toy}}[t]=-\frac{1}{4}
we can therefore rewrite Eq. (
5.13
) as

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{toy}}=\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{toy}}[q_{1}]+\frac{1}{2}D^{2}_{\mathcal{V}}|_{0}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{toy}}(b_{1})+\frac{c_{\mathrm{toy}}}{N}.

Definition 5.5

(Taylor approximation of the square root)
.

Let
\eta_{m}
be the function defined in Eq. (
5.7
) and let us define the constant
c_{m}:=\frac{m}{8}d
. We then define the residuum corresponding to the operator Taylor approximation of
\Big(1-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\Big)^{\frac{m}{2}}
, for different degrees of accuracy, as

\displaystyle E_{m}^{0}:

\displaystyle=\Big(1-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\Big)^{\frac{m}{2}}-\eta_{m}\left(q\right),

\displaystyle E_{m}^{1}:

\displaystyle=\Big(1-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\Big)^{\frac{m}{2}}-\eta_{m}\left(q\right)-D_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{q}\eta_{m}\big(b_{\geq 1}\big),

\displaystyle E_{m}^{2}:

\displaystyle=\Big(1-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\Big)^{\frac{m}{2}}-\eta_{m}\left(q\right)-D_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{q}\eta_{m}\big(b_{\geq 1}\big)-\frac{1}{2}D^{2}_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{0}\eta_{m}\big(b_{\geq 1}\big)-\frac{c_{m}}{N}.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
J=(i,j)\in\{0,\dots,4\}^{2}
be such that
\lambda_{J}\neq 0
, where
\lambda_{J}
is defined in Lemma
5.3
, and let
R_{J}
be the residuum defined in Eq. (
5.11
). By distinguishing different cases with the help of the index
e_{J}:=\left|\{\ell\in J:\ell\in\{3,4\}\}\right|
and the index
m_{J}:=\left|\{\ell\in J:\ell=0\}\right|
, we can explicitly express
R_{J}
as

•

In the case
m_{J}=2
, i.e.
J=(0,0)
:
R_{(0,0)}=\left(u_{0}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot u_{0}\right)E_{2}^{2}
.

•

In the case
e_{J}=0
and
m_{J}<2
:
R_{J}=\left(h_{i}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot h_{j}\right)E_{m_{J}}^{1}
.

•

In the case
e_{J}=1
, there exists a constant
C
and functions
F_{J}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
|F_{J}(t)|\leq C|t|
, such that

\displaystyle R_{J}=\left(h_{i}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot h_{j}\right)E_{m_{J}}^{0}+\frac{F_{J}(q)}{N}.

(5.14)

•

For
e_{J}=2
we distinguish further between the individual cases and obtain

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ R_{(3,3)}

\displaystyle=\left(ip^{\prime}-ip\right)^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot ip^{\prime}+(ip)^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot\left(ip^{\prime}-ip\right),

\displaystyle R_{(3,4)}

\displaystyle=\left(ip^{\prime}-ip\right)^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot b_{>d}=R_{(4,3)}^{\dagger},

\displaystyle R_{(4,4)}

\displaystyle=0.

Proof.

The Lemma can be verified by straightforward computations for the different individual cases. For the purpose of illustration, we will explicitly carry out the computations for the case
J=(3,j)
with
j\in\{0,1,2\}
, i.e. we are going to verify Eq. (
5.14
) for this special case. Using the definition of
E^{0}_{m}
in Definition
5.5
, the observation
h_{j}=e_{j}(q)
and the fact that
\left(ip^{\prime}_{\ell}\right)^{\dagger}=b_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot\big(u_{\ell}-\partial_{u_{\ell}}f(q)\big)-\big(u_{\ell}-\partial_{u_{\ell}}f(q)\big)^{\dagger}\cdot b_{\geq 1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle H_{J}=\left(ip^{\prime}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot e_{j}(q)\left(1-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{m}{2}}=\left(ip^{\prime}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot e_{j}(q)\eta_{m}(q)+\left(ip^{\prime}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot e_{j}(q)E^{0}_{m}

\displaystyle\ \ =\frac{1}{2}\!\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}b_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\!\cdot\!\big(u_{\ell}\!-\!\partial_{u_{\ell}}f(q)\!\big)u_{\ell}^{\dagger}\!\cdot\!T\!\cdot\!e_{j}(q)\,\eta_{m}(q)-\frac{1}{2}\!\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\big(u_{\ell}\!-\!\partial_{u_{\ell}}f(q)\!\big)^{\dagger}\!\cdot\!b_{\geq 1}u_{\ell}^{\dagger}\!\cdot\!T\!\cdot\!e_{j}(q)\,\eta_{m}(q)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\left(ip^{\prime}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot e_{j}(q)E^{0}_{m},

(5.15)

where
m:=m_{J}
. Our goal is to commute
b_{\geq 1}
in
\big(u_{\ell}-\partial_{u_{\ell}}f(q)\big)^{\dagger}\cdot b_{\geq 1}u_{\ell}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot e_{j}(q)\,\eta_{m}(q)
to the right side, in order to obtain an expression which is of the same form as (
5.10
). We define the corresponding functions
w
and
\widetilde{w}
as

\displaystyle w:=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\left(u_{\ell}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot e_{j}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)\!\eta_{m}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)\right)\big(u_{\ell}-\partial_{t_{\ell}}f(t)\big)

and
\widetilde{w}(t):=-w(t)
. The commutation law
\left[g(q),\big(u_{\ell}\!-\!\partial_{u_{\ell}}f(q)\!\big)^{\dagger}\!\cdot\!b_{\geq 1}\right]=\left[g(q),ip_{\ell}\right]=-\frac{1}{2N}\partial_{\ell}g(q)
, for
C^{1}
functions
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
then yields

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\big(u_{\ell}-\partial_{u_{\ell}}f(q)\big)^{\dagger}\!\cdot\!b_{\geq 1}\,u_{\ell}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot e_{j}(q)\,\eta_{m}(q)=w(q)^{\dagger}\cdot b_{\geq 1}+\frac{1}{N}y(q),

where
y:\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined as
y(t):=-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\ell}\partial_{\ell}\left(u_{\ell}\cdot T\cdot e_{j}(\vec{t}\ )\eta_{m}(\vec{t}\ )\right)
. Furthermore

\displaystyle D_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{\vec{t}}\ \mathcal{E}_{J}(z)\!

\displaystyle=\!e_{3}(z)^{\dagger}\!\cdot\!T\!\cdot\!e_{j}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)\!\eta_{m}\!\left(\vec{t}\ \right)\!=\!\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\!\left(i\mathfrak{Im}\left[\!\big(u_{\ell}\!-\!\partial_{t_{\ell}}f(t)\big)^{\dagger}\!\cdot\!z\!\right]u_{\ell}\right)^{\dagger}\!\cdot\!T\!\cdot\!e_{j}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)\!\eta_{m}\!\left(\vec{t}\ \right)

\displaystyle=w(t)^{\dagger}\cdot z+z^{\dagger}\cdot\widetilde{w}(t).

Consequently we can rewrite Eq. (
5.1
) as

\displaystyle\left(ip^{\prime}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot e_{j}(q)\left(1-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{m}{2}}

\displaystyle=D_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{q}\ \mathcal{E}_{J}\left(b_{\geq 1}\right)+\frac{1}{N}y(q)+\left(ip^{\prime}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot e_{j}(q)E^{0}_{m}.

Note that
\mathcal{E}_{J}\left(\vec{t}\,\right)=0
and
D^{2}_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{0}\mathcal{E}_{J}=0
. Therefore Eq. (
5.14
) follows from the fact that
F(t):=y(t)-c_{J}
is Lipschitz and
F(0)=0
, which implies that there exists a constant
C
such that
|F(t)|\leq C|t|
.
∎

For the proof of the following Theorem, we will use various operator estimates derived in Appendices
C
and
D
.

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
J\in\{0,\dots,4\}^{2}
be such that
\lambda_{J}\neq 0
and let
R_{J}
be the residuum defined in Eq. (
5.11
). Then,

\displaystyle R_{J}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right),

with
\mathbb{T}_{N}
defined in Eq. (
4.11
) and the
o_{*}(\cdot)
notation from Definition
4.10
.

Proof.

Recall the definitions in Lemma
5.6
of
e_{J}:=\left|\{l\in J:l\in\{3,4\}\}\right|
, which counts how many of the indices in
J=(i,j)
are equal to
3
or
4
,
m_{J}:=\left|\{l\in J:l=0\}\right|
, which counts how many of the indices are zero, and the residuum
R_{J}
defined in Eq. (
5.11
). In order to prove the statement of the Theorem, we are going to verify
R_{J}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
for all
J
with
\lambda_{J}\neq 0
.

The case
J=(0,0)
: In this case we have the identity
R_{(0,0)}=\left(u_{0}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot u_{0}\right)E_{2}^{2}
, hence we have to verify
E_{2}^{2}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
. In order to do this, recall the function
\eta_{2}(x)=1-\|F(x)\|^{2}
from Eq. (
5.7
) and let us compute using Lemma
4.9

\displaystyle 1-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=1-\left(q+f(q)+ip^{\prime}+b_{>d}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\left(q+f(q)+ip^{\prime}+b_{>d}\right)

\displaystyle=1-q^{\dagger}\cdot q-f(q)^{\dagger}\cdot f(q)-f(q)^{\dagger}\cdot b_{>d}-b_{>d}^{\dagger}\cdot f(q)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ -b_{>d}^{\dagger}\cdot b_{>d}-\left(p^{\dagger}\cdot p\right)+\frac{d}{2N}-p^{\dagger}\cdot\left(p^{\prime}-p\right)-\left(p^{\prime}-p\right)^{\dagger}\cdot p^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\eta_{2}(q)+D_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{q}\eta_{2}\big(b_{\geq 1}\big)+D^{2}_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{0}\eta_{2}\big(b_{\geq 1}\big)+\frac{d}{4N}-p^{\dagger}\cdot\left(p^{\prime}-p\right)-\left(p^{\prime}-p\right)^{\dagger}\cdot p^{\prime},

where we used
\eta_{2}(q)=1-q^{\dagger}\cdot q-f(q)^{\dagger}\cdot f(q)
and
p^{\dagger}\cdot p=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(2b_{j}^{\dagger}b_{j}-b_{j}^{2}-\left(b_{j}^{\dagger}\right)^{2}\right)+\frac{d}{4N}
. Note that
\frac{c_{2}}{N}=\frac{d}{4N}
, where
c_{2}
is the constant from Definition
5.5
. Since
p^{2}\leq\mathbb{T}_{N}
, it is clear that
p^{2}=O_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
. In Lemmata
C.6
and
C.5
, we will verify that
\left(p^{\prime}\right)^{2}=O_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
and
\left(p^{\prime}-p\right)^{2}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
. Therefore we obtain by the operator Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in the auxiliary Lemma
C.1
that
p^{\dagger}\cdot\left(p^{\prime}-p\right)
as well as
\left(p^{\prime}-p\right)^{\dagger}\cdot p^{\prime}
are of order
o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
. We conclude
E_{2}^{2}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
.

The case
e_{J}=0
, with
J\neq(0,0)
: In this case
m_{J}\in\{0,1\}
and the error is given by

\displaystyle R_{J}=h_{i}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot h_{j}\,E^{1}_{m_{J}}=e_{i}\left(q\right)^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot e_{j}\left(q\right)\,E^{1}_{m_{J}}.

We clearly have
E_{0}^{1}=0
. For
m_{J}=1
, let us define the function
V(t):=e_{i}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot e_{j}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)
, which satisfies
V(t)\leq C|t|
for a constant
C
. In Lemma
D.2
we will then verify that
V(q)E^{1}_{1}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
.

The case
e_{J}=1
: In this case the error reads
R_{J}=\left(h^{\dagger}_{i}\cdot T\cdot h_{j}\right)\,E^{0}_{m_{J}}+\frac{F_{J}(q)}{N}
, where
F_{J}(t)\leq C|t|
for some constant
C
. Using Lemma
D.2
and Lemma
C.4
from the Appendix, we obtain that
\left(E^{0}_{1}\right)^{\dagger}E^{0}_{1}=o_{*}\left(T_{N}\right)
and
\frac{F_{J}(q)}{N}=o_{*}\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)
. Regarding the first term, note that
E^{0}_{0}=0
. Hence, we assume w.l.o.g.
m_{J}=1
. We are done once we can verify

\displaystyle\left(h^{\dagger}_{i}\cdot T\cdot h_{j}\right)\cdot\left(h^{\dagger}_{i}\cdot T\cdot h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}=O_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)

(5.16)

in case one of the indices
i,j
is in
\{3,4\}
and the other is zero. Let us first assume
i\in\{3,4\}
. Then
h^{\dagger}_{i}\cdot T\cdot h_{j}=h_{i}^{\dagger}\cdot w
, with
w:=T\cdot u_{0}\in\mathcal{H}
, and therefore Eq. (
5.16
) follows from Lemma
C.6
in the case
i=3
and from Lemma
C.5
in the case
i=4
. The proof of the case
j\in\{3,4\}
follows analogously.

The case
e_{J}=2
: In this case, the error is a linear combination of
\left(ip^{\prime}-ip\right)^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot h_{j}
and
h_{i}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot\left(ip^{\prime}-ip\right)
with
h_{i},h_{j}\in\{p^{\prime},b_{>d}\}
. Note that
A:=\sqrt{T}\left(1_{\mathcal{H}}-\pi_{>d}\right)
is bounded, and therefore

\displaystyle\left(ip^{\prime}-ip\right)^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot\left(ip^{\prime}-ip\right)=\left(ip^{\prime}-ip\right)^{\dagger}\cdot A^{\dagger}A\cdot\left(ip^{\prime}-ip\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \leq\|A\|^{2}\ \left(ip^{\prime}-ip\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\left(ip^{\prime}-ip\right)=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)

by Lemma
C.5
. Similarly, we have
\left(p^{\prime}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot p^{\prime}\leq\|A\|^{2}(p^{\prime})^{\dagger}\cdot p^{\prime}=O_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
by Lemma
C.6
. Hence Lemma
C.1
tells us that
\left(ip^{\prime}-ip\right)^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot h_{j}
and
h_{i}^{\dagger}\cdot T\cdot\left(ip^{\prime}-ip\right)
are of order
o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

Recall the functional
\mathcal{E}_{A}
defined in Eq. (
5.9
) and let us define the constant
c:=\sum_{J\in\{0,\dots,4\}^{2}}\lambda_{J}c_{J}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{N}\widetilde{A}_{N}\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{A}(q)+D_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{q}\mathcal{E}_{A}\left(b_{\geq 1}\right)+\frac{1}{2}D^{2}_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{0}\mathcal{E}_{A}\left(b_{\geq 1}\right)+\frac{c}{N}+o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right).

Proof.

The statement follows from combining Lemma
5.3
and Theorem
5.7
.
∎

5.2
Taylor Expansion of
\mathcal{W}_{N}\widetilde{B}_{N}\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}

Similar to the previous subsection, we introduce atoms
H_{J}
in Definition
5.9
as well as their classical counterparts
\mathcal{E}_{J}
in Definition
5.10
. In Lemma
5.11
we explain how
\mathcal{W}_{N}\widetilde{B}_{N}\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{B}
can be written in terms of
H_{J}
and
\mathcal{E}_{J}
, respectively.

Definition 5.9
.

Recall the definition of
h_{i}:\mathrm{dom}[\mathcal{N}]\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{0}\otimes\mathcal{H}
from Definition
5.1
. For a multi-index
J=(i,j,k,\ell)
with
i,j,k,\ell\in\{0,\dots,4\}
, we define an operator
H_{J}
on
\mathcal{W}_{N}\mathcal{F}_{\leq N}
as

\displaystyle H_{J}:

\displaystyle=\left(h_{i}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot h_{k}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{\ell}\left(1-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\right)^{\frac{m_{J}}{2}},

where
m_{J}
counts how many of the indices
i,j,k,\ell
are zero.

Definition 5.10
.

Recall the definition of
e_{i}:\mathcal{H}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}
and
\eta_{m}
from Definition
5.2
. For a multi-index
J=(i,j,k,\ell)
with
i,j,k,\ell\in\{0,\dots,4\}
, we define
\mathcal{E}_{J}:\mathcal{H}_{0}\cap\mathrm{dom}[T]\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{J}(z):

\displaystyle=\Big[e_{i}(z)\otimes e_{j}(z)\Big]^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot e_{k}(z)\otimes e_{\ell}(z)\ \eta_{m_{J}}(z),

where
m_{J}
counts how many of the indices
i,j,k,\ell
are zero and
\eta_{m}
are the functions defined in Eq. (
5.7
).

Lemma 5.11
.

Let us define for all
i,j,k,\ell\in\{1,\dots,4\}
the coefficients
\lambda_{(0,0,0,0)}:=\frac{1}{2}
,
\lambda_{(i,0,0,0)}:=2
,
\lambda_{(i,j,0,0)}:=\lambda_{(i,0,k,0)}:=\lambda_{(0,j,k,0)}:=1
,
\lambda_{(i,j,k,0)}:=2
,
\lambda_{(i,j,k,\ell)}:=\frac{1}{2}
and all other coefficients are defined as
\lambda_{J}:=0
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{N}\,\widetilde{B}_{N}\,\mathcal{W}_{N}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\in\{0,\dots,4\}^{4}}\lambda_{J}\ \mathfrak{Re}\left[H_{J}\right].

Furthermore, the functional
\mathcal{E}_{B}
defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{B}(z)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{J\in\{0,\dots,4\}^{4}}\lambda_{J}\ \mathfrak{Re}\left[\mathcal{E}_{J}(z)\right],

(5.17)

is an extension of
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{B}\big|_{B_{r}}
defined in Eq. (
5.6
), where
B_{r}:=\{z\in\mathcal{H}_{0}\cap\mathrm{dom}\left[T\right]:\|z\|<r\}
and
r>0
is a constant such that
\|F(z)\|<\frac{1}{2}
for all
z\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
with
\|z\|<r
.

The proof of Lemma
5.11
works analogously to the proof of Lemma
5.3
. Following the strategy from Subsection
5.1
we are going to verify that the residuum
R_{J}

\displaystyle R_{J}:

\displaystyle=H_{J}-\mathcal{E}_{J}(q)-D_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{q}\mathcal{E}_{J}\left(b_{\geq 1}\right)-\frac{1}{2}D^{2}_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{0}\mathcal{E}_{J}\left(b_{\geq 1}\right)-\frac{c_{J}}{N}

(5.18)

is small, where the constant
c_{J}
are given by
c_{(0,0,0,0)}:=-\frac{1}{8}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\partial^{2}_{t_{j}}\big|_{t=0}\mathcal{E}_{(0,0,0,0)}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)
and

\displaystyle c_{(3,3,0,0)}:

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{8}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\partial_{t_{j}}^{2}\big|_{t=0}\mathcal{E}_{(1,1,0,0)}\left(\vec{t}\ \right),\ \ c_{(3,1,0,0)}:=-c_{(3,3,0,0)},\ \ \ c_{(1,3,0,0)}:=c_{(3,3,0,0)},

\displaystyle c_{(3,0,3,0)}:

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\partial_{t_{j}}^{2}\big|_{t=0}\mathcal{E}_{(1,0,1,0)}\left(\vec{t}\ \right),\ \ \ \ c_{(1,0,3,0)}:=-c_{(3,0,3,0)},\ \ \ c_{(3,0,1,0)}:=-c_{(3,0,3,0)},

\displaystyle c_{(0,3,3,0)}:

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\partial_{t_{j}}^{2}\big|_{t=0}\mathcal{E}_{(0,1,1,0)}\left(\vec{t}\ \right),\ \ \ \ c_{(0,1,3,0)}:=-c_{(0,3,3,0)},\ \ \ c_{(0,3,1,0)}:=-c_{(0,3,3,0)},

(5.19)

and all other constants are defined as
c_{J}:=0
. The proof will be split into two parts. In Lemma
5.12
we derive an explicit representation of the residuum
R_{J}
by sorting the operator
H_{J}
in terms of powers in
p
and
b_{>d}
, and in Theorem
5.16
we will make sure that this residuum is indeed small compared to the operator
\mathbb{T}_{N}
.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
J=(i,j,k,\ell)\in\{0,\dots,4\}^{4}
be such that
\lambda_{J}\neq 0
, where
\lambda_{J}
is defined in Lemma
5.11
, and let
R_{J}
be the residuum defined in Eq. (
5.18
). By distinguishing different cases with the help of the indices
e_{J}:=\left|\{\ell\in J:\ell\in\{3,4\}\}\right|
and
m_{J}:=\left|\{\ell\in J:\ell=0\}\right|
, we can explicitly express
R_{J}
as:

•

In the case
m_{J}=4
, i.e.
J=(0,0,0,0)
:
R_{J}=\left(h_{i}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot h_{k}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{\ell}\,E_{4}^{2}
.

•

In the case
e_{J}=0
and
m_{J}<4
:
R_{J}=\left(h_{i}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot h_{k}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{\ell}\,E_{m_{J}}^{1}
.

•

In the case
e_{J}=1
, there exists a constant
C
and functions
F_{J}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
|F_{J}(t)|\leq C|t|
, such that
R_{J}=\left(h_{i}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot h_{k}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{\ell}\,E_{m_{J}}^{0}+\frac{F_{J}(q)}{N}
.

•

In the case
e_{J}=2
and
m_{J}=2
when two of the indices are
4
:

\displaystyle R_{J}=-\left(h_{i}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot h_{k}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{\ell}\,\mathbb{L}^{\prime}.\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

•

In the case
e_{J}=2
and
m_{J}=2
when one of the indices is
3
and another one is
4
, let us define
\widetilde{h}_{3}:=p^{\prime}-p
and
\widetilde{h}_{r}:=h_{r}
for
r\in\{0,1,2,4\}
. Then,

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ R_{J}=-\left(h_{i}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot h_{k}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{\ell}\,\mathbb{L}^{\prime}+\left(\widetilde{h}_{i}\ \underline{\otimes}\ \widetilde{h}_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot\widetilde{h}_{k}\ \underline{\otimes}\ \widetilde{h}_{\ell}.

•

In the case
e_{J}=2
and
m_{J}=2
when two of the indices are
3
, let us define the coefficients
\Lambda_{(3,3,0,0)}^{r,r^{\prime}}:=-\left(u_{r}\otimes u_{r^{\prime}}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot u_{0}\otimes u_{0}
,
\Lambda_{(3,0,3,0)}^{r,r^{\prime}}:=\left(u_{r}\otimes u_{0}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot u_{r^{\prime}}\otimes u_{0}
and
\Lambda_{(0,3,3,0)}^{r,r^{\prime}}:=\left(u_{0}\otimes u_{r}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot u_{r^{\prime}}\otimes u_{0}
. Then,

\displaystyle R_{J}\!=

\displaystyle\!-\!\left(h_{i}\,\underline{\otimes}\,h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot h_{k}\,\underline{\otimes}\,h_{\ell}\,\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\!+\!\sum_{r,r^{\prime}=1}^{d}\!\Lambda_{J}^{r,r^{\prime}}\left[(p^{\prime}_{r}\!-\!p_{r})\cdot p^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}\!\!+p_{r}\cdot(p^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}\!-\!p_{r^{\prime}})\right].

(5.20)

•

In the cases
e_{J}=2
and
m_{J}<2
, respectively
e_{J}>2
:
R_{J}=H_{J}
.

Proof.

Similar to the proof of Lemma
5.6
, the proof of Lemma
5.12
follows from a straightforward computation for the individual cases. For the purpose of illustration, we will explicitly carry out the computations for the case
J=(3,0,3,0)
, i.e. we are going to verify Eq. (
5.20
). Since
\mathcal{E}_{(3,0,3,0)}(z)
is quadratic in
\pi(z)
, we immediately obtain
\mathcal{E}_{(3,0,3,0)}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)=0
and
D_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{\vec{t}}\ \mathcal{E}_{(3,0,3,0)}=0
. Let us define the coefficients
\lambda_{\alpha,\gamma}:=\left(u_{\alpha}\otimes u_{0}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot u_{\gamma}\otimes u_{0}
, the operator
Q=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\gamma=1}^{d}\hat{v}_{\alpha 0,\gamma 0}\ u_{\alpha}\cdot u_{\gamma}^{\dagger}
and
G\in\mathcal{H}_{0}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{0}
by
G=-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\alpha,\gamma=1}^{d}\hat{v}_{\alpha 0,\gamma 0}\ u_{\alpha}\otimes u_{\gamma}
. Then

\displaystyle D^{2}_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{0}\ \mathcal{E}_{(3,0,3,0)}(z)=z^{\dagger}\cdot Q\cdot z+G^{\dagger}\cdot z\otimes z+\left(z\otimes z\right)\cdot G

and therefore
D^{2}_{\mathcal{V}}\big|_{0}\ \mathcal{E}_{(3,0,3,0)}\left(b_{\geq 1}\right)=b_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot Q\cdot b_{\geq 1}+G^{\dagger}\cdot b_{\geq 1}\otimes b_{\geq 1}+\left(b_{\geq 1}\otimes b_{\geq 1}\right)\cdot G
. This concludes the proof of Eq. (
5.20
), since

\displaystyle H_{J}-\left(ip^{\prime}\ \underline{\otimes}\ u_{0}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot ip^{\prime}\ \underline{\otimes}\ u_{0}\left(-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\right)-\sum_{r,r^{\prime}=1}^{d}\Lambda_{J}^{r,r^{\prime}}\left[(p^{\prime}_{r}-p_{r})\cdot p^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}+p_{r}\cdot(p^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}-p_{r^{\prime}})\right]

\displaystyle\ =\left(ip\ \underline{\otimes}\ u_{0}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot ip\ \underline{\otimes}\ u_{0}\!=\!\sum_{\alpha,\gamma=1}^{d}\!\left(u_{\alpha}\otimes u_{0}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot u_{\gamma}\otimes u_{0}\ \frac{1}{2}\left(b_{\alpha}-b_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\left(b_{\gamma}-b_{\gamma}^{\dagger}\right)

\displaystyle\ =b_{\geq 1}^{\dagger}\cdot Q\cdot b_{\geq 1}+\left(b_{\geq 1}\ \underline{\otimes}\ b_{\geq 1}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot G+G^{\dagger}\cdot b_{\geq 1}\ \underline{\otimes}\ b_{\geq 1}+\frac{c_{(3,0,3,0)}}{N}.

∎

In the remainder of this subsection, we are going to verify that the residuum
R_{J}
is small compared to the quadratic operator
\mathbb{T}_{N}
. Note that the error term in the last case of Lemma
5.12
is quite different from the other cases, since it simply corresponds to the whole operator
H_{J}
. This is not surprising, however, since the second order Taylor approximation of an object that is already of an higher order than two is zero, i.e. the residuum coincides with the object itself. With the help of the following three results in Lemma
5.13
, Lemma
5.14
and Theorem
5.15
, we will systematically verify that
H_{J}
is small compared to the quadratic operator
\mathbb{T}_{N}
in the cases
e_{J}=2
and
m_{J}<2
, respectively
e_{J}>2
. Regarding all other cases, we will verify the smallness of the residuum in Theorem
5.16
. In order to do this, we will repeatedly use results derived in Appendices
C
and
D
.

Lemma 5.13
.

For indices
i,j\in\{0,\dots,4\}
, we have the following estimates:

•

In case one of the indices is contained in
\{3,4\}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(h_{i}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot|\hat{v}|\cdot h_{i}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}=O_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right).

•

In case one of the indices is contained in
\{3,4\}
and the other one is contained in
\{1,\dots,4\}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(h_{i}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot|\hat{v}|\cdot h_{i}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right).

Proof.

We will repeatedly use the inequality
|v|\leq\Lambda\left(T+1\right)=:S
from Assumption
1.1
, which implies together with the translation-invariance of
T
the inequalities
|\hat{v}|\leq S\otimes 1_{\mathcal{H}}
and
|\hat{v}|\leq\ 1_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes S
.

The case
i\in\{1,2\}
and
j\in\{3,4\}
: Recall that
h_{k}=e_{k}(q)
for
k\in\{0,1,2\}
and let us define the function
\phi(t):=e_{i}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)^{\dagger}\cdot S\cdot e_{i}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)
. Using the inequality
|\hat{v}|\leq S\otimes 1_{\mathcal{H}}
we obtain

\displaystyle\left(e_{i}(q)\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot|\hat{v}|\cdot e_{i}(q)\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\leq h_{j}^{\dagger}\cdot\phi(q)\cdot h_{j}.

Since
|\phi(t)|\leq C\left(|t|+|t|^{2}\right)
for a constant
C
, we obtain
h_{3}^{\dagger}\cdot\phi(q)\cdot h_{3}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
and
h_{4}^{\dagger}\cdot\phi(q)\cdot h_{4}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
by Lemmata
C.4
and
C.6
.

The case
i\in\{3,4\}
and
j\in\{1,2\}
: Making use of the commutation laws
[b_{\alpha},q_{\beta}]=0
and
[ip_{\alpha},q_{\beta}]=\frac{1}{2N}\delta_{\alpha,\beta}
, this case follows from the previous one.

The case
i=3,j=3
: Let
\pi_{\leq d}:=\sum_{r=1}^{d}u_{r}\cdot u_{r}^{\dagger}
. Since
\left((p^{\prime})^{\dagger}\cdot p^{\prime}\right)^{2}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(p^{\prime}\ \underline{\otimes}\ p^{\prime}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot|\hat{v}|\cdot p^{\prime}\ \underline{\otimes}\ p^{\prime}\leq(p^{\prime})^{\dagger}\cdot\left((p^{\prime})^{\dagger}\cdot p^{\prime}\right)\otimes S\cdot p^{\prime}\leq\big\|\pi_{\leq d}\,S\,\pi_{\leq d}\big\|\left((p^{\prime})^{\dagger}\cdot p^{\prime}\right)^{2}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right).

The case
i=4
and
j\in\{3,4\}
: Note that

\displaystyle\left(b_{>d}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot|\hat{v}|

\displaystyle\cdot b_{>d}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\leq 2\left(f(q)\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot|\hat{v}|\cdot f(q)\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}

\displaystyle+2\left((b_{>d}+f(q))\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot|\hat{v}|\cdot(b_{>d}+f(q))\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}

By the previous case
i\in\{1,2\}
and
j\in\{3,4\}
, we know that
\left(f(q)\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot|\hat{v}|\cdot f(q)\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
. For the second contribution, recall the definition of
\pi_{M,N}
from Remark
4.11
and let
\hat{\pi}_{M,N}
be the orthogonal projection onto the subspace
\mathcal{W}_{N}\mathcal{F}^{+}_{\leq M}\subset\mathcal{F}_{0}
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{+}_{\leq M}:=\mathds{1}_{[0,M]}\left(\sum_{j>d}^{\infty}a_{j}^{\dagger}\cdot a_{j}\right).

(5.21)

Since we have
b_{k}\,\hat{\pi}_{M,N}=\hat{\pi}_{M,N}\,b_{k}\,\hat{\pi}_{M,N}
for
k>d
and
[p^{\prime}_{j},\hat{\pi}_{M,N}]=0
by Lemma
C.3
, we obtain using
\pi_{M,N}=\hat{\pi}_{M,N}\,\pi_{M,N}
(which follows from
\mathcal{W}_{N}\mathcal{F}_{\leq M}\subset\mathcal{W}_{N}\mathcal{F}^{+}_{\leq M}
)

\displaystyle\pi_{M,N}\left((b_{>d}+f(q))\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot|\hat{v}|\cdot(b_{>d}+f(q))\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\,\pi_{M,N}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \leq\pi_{M,N}\,h_{j}^{\dagger}\cdot\left(\hat{\pi}_{M,N}\,(b_{>d}+f(q))^{\dagger}\cdot(b_{>d}+f(q))\,\hat{\pi}_{M,N}\right)\otimes S\cdot h_{j}\,\pi_{M,N}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \leq\frac{M}{N}\ \pi_{M,N}\,h_{j}^{\dagger}\cdot S\cdot h_{j}\,\pi_{M,N}\leq C\ \frac{M}{N}\ \pi_{M,N}\,\mathbb{T}_{N}\,\pi_{M,N}

for a constant
0<C<\infty
, where we used
h_{j}^{\dagger}\cdot S\cdot h_{j}=O_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
and and the characterization of the
O_{*}(\cdot)
notation in Remark
4.11
for the last inequality. Using this characterization for the inequality above yields
\left((b_{>d}+f(q))\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot|\hat{v}|\cdot(b_{>d}+f(q))\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
.

The case
i=3
and
j=4
: Making use of the commutation laws
[ip^{\prime}_{\alpha},b]=-\frac{1}{2N}\partial_{\alpha}f(q)
, this case follows from the previous one.

The case
i=0
and
j\in\{3,4\}
, respectively
i\in\{3,4\}
and
j=0
: Since
h_{0}=1_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}\otimes u_{0}
commutes with
h_{3}=ip^{\prime}
and
h_{4}=b_{>d}
, we assume w.l.o.g.
i=0
and
j\in\{3,4\}
. With
\lambda:=u_{0}^{\dagger}\cdot S\cdot u_{0}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(u_{0}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot|\hat{v}|\cdot u_{0}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\leq\lambda\ h_{j}^{\dagger}\cdot h_{j}=O_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right).

∎

Lemma 5.14
.

In the following, let
G:\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
be a differentiable function and let us define the operators
X,Y:\mathrm{dom}[\mathcal{N}]\longrightarrow{\mathcal{F}_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{H}
as

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle:=\left(ip^{\prime}\right)^{\dagger}\otimes 1_{\mathcal{H}}\cdot G(q)=-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}iG_{j,k}p^{\prime}_{j}\right)\otimes u_{k},

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle:=b_{>d}^{\dagger}\otimes 1_{\mathcal{H}}\cdot G(q)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{j>d}iG_{j,k}b^{\dagger}_{j}\right)\otimes u_{k}.

Then we have the estimates

\displaystyle X^{\dagger}\cdot X

\displaystyle\leq 2\left[\left(p^{\prime}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\|G\|^{2}(q)\cdot p^{\prime}+\frac{d}{N^{2}}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{d}\|\partial_{\alpha}G\|^{2}(q)\right],

\displaystyle Y^{\dagger}\cdot Y

\displaystyle\leq b_{>d}^{\dagger}\cdot\|G\|^{2}(q)\cdot b_{>d}+\frac{1}{N}\|G\|^{2}(q).

The proof of Lemma
5.14
is based on the commutation relations
[b_{\alpha},b_{\beta}^{\dagger}]=\frac{1}{N}\delta_{\alpha,\beta}
and
[p_{\alpha},q_{\beta}]=\frac{1}{2iN}\delta_{\alpha,\beta}
, and is left to the reader.

Theorem 5.15
.

Let
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
be the operator from Definition
4.8
. Then we have the following estimates:

•

In case at least two of the indices
i,j,k,\ell\in\{1,\dots,4\}
are contained in
\{3,4\}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(h_{i}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot h_{k}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{\ell}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right),

•

In case at least two of the indices
i,j,k\in\{0,\dots,4\}
are contained in
\{3,4\}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(h_{i}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot h_{k}\ \underline{\otimes}\ u_{0}\,\mathbb{L}^{\prime}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right),

•

In case at least two of the indices
i,j,k\in\{1,\dots,4\}
are contained in
\{3,4\}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(h_{i}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot h_{k}\ \underline{\otimes}\ u_{0}\,\sqrt{1-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right).

Proof.

Let us denote with
e_{(a,b)}
the number of indices in
(a,b)
that are elements of
\{3,4\}
. In the following, we will verify the theorem separately for the case
e_{(i,j)}\geq 1
and
e_{(k,\ell)}\geq 1
, and the case
e_{(k,\ell)}=0
. Note that the case
e_{(i,j)}=0
is only possible for the first bullet point, and the proof of the statement follows from the case
e_{(k,\ell)}=0
, since

\displaystyle\left[\left(h_{i}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot h_{k}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{\ell}\right]^{\dagger}=\left(h_{k}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{\ell}\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot h_{i}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}.

The case
e_{(i,j)}\geq 1
and
e_{(k,\ell)}\geq 1
:
Let us define the operators
A:=h_{i}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{j}
and
Q:=\hat{v}
, and depending on the concrete bullet point let us define
B
as
h_{k}\ \underline{\otimes}\ h_{\ell}
,
h_{k}\ \underline{\otimes}\ u_{0}\,\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
or
h_{k}\ \underline{\otimes}\ u_{0}\,\sqrt{1-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}}
. In any case we have to verify

\displaystyle A^{\dagger}\cdot Q\cdot B=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right).

By Lemma
C.1
, it is enough to verify that one of the operators
A^{\dagger}\cdot|Q|\cdot A
and
B^{\dagger}\cdot|Q|\cdot B
is of order
o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
, and the other one is of order
O_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
, which follows from Lemma
5.13
and the auxiliary Corollary
D.4
.

The case
e_{(k,\ell)}=0
: In this case we have
i,j\in\{3,4\}
for any of the bullet points. Let us define the function
G:\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
by
G(t):=1_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes\left(T+1\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot e_{k}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)\otimes e_{\ell}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)
. Note that
G(t)\in\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
follows from Assumption
1.1
. We define the operator
X:=\left(T+1\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot h_{i}
and depending on the concrete bullet point let us define
Y:=h^{\dagger}_{j}\otimes 1_{\mathcal{H}}\cdot G(q)\,Z
with
Z:=1_{\mathcal{F}_{0}}
,
Z:=\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
or
Z:=\sqrt{1-\mathbb{L}^{\prime}}
. In the following, we have to verify
X^{\dagger}\cdot Y=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
. Since
i\in\{3,4\}
, we know that
X^{\dagger}\cdot X=O_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
. By the Cauchy–Schwarz like result in Lemma
C.1
, it is therefore enough to verify
Y^{\dagger}\cdot Y=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
. Applying Lemma
5.14
yields in any case

\displaystyle Y^{\dagger}\cdot Y

\displaystyle=Z^{\dagger}\left(h^{\dagger}_{j}\otimes 1_{\mathcal{H}}\cdot G(q)\right)^{\dagger}\cdot\left(h^{\dagger}_{j}\otimes 1_{\mathcal{H}}\cdot G(q)\right)Z

\displaystyle\leq 2\ Z^{\dagger}\left[h_{j}^{\dagger}\cdot\|G(q)\|^{2}\cdot h_{j}+\frac{1}{N}\|G(q)\|^{2}+\frac{d}{N^{2}}\sum_{r=1}^{d}\|\partial_{r}G(q)\|^{2}\right]Z,

and Corollary
D.4
then yields that
Z^{\dagger}\,\|G(q)\|^{2}\,Z
and
Z^{\dagger}\,\frac{1}{N}\left(\sum_{r=1}^{d}\|\partial_{r}G(q)\|^{2}\right)Z
are of order
o_{*}(1)
. Therefore,
Z^{\dagger}\,\frac{1}{N}\|G(q)\|^{2}Z
and
Z^{\dagger}\,\frac{d}{N^{2}}\left(\sum_{r=1}^{d}\|\partial_{r}G(q)\|^{2}\right)Z
are both of order
o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
. Finally,
Z^{\dagger}\,h_{j}^{\dagger}\cdot\|G(q)\|^{2}\cdot h_{j}\,Z=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
follows from the auxiliary Lemmata
C.4
and
C.6
, and the auxiliary Corollary
D.4
.
∎

Theorem 5.16
.

Let
J\in\{0,\dots,4\}^{4}
be such that
\lambda_{J}\neq 0
and let
R_{J}
be the residuum defined in Eq. (
5.18
). Then,

\displaystyle R_{J}

\displaystyle=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right).

Proof.

Let
J=(i,j,k,\ell)
be a multi index with
\lambda_{J}\neq 0
, and recall the index
e_{J}:=\left|\{l\in J:l\in\{3,4\}\}\right|
and the index
m_{J}:=\left|\{l\in J:l=0\}\right|
from Lemma
5.12
as well as the residuum defined in Eq. (
5.18
). In order to prove the statement of the Theorem, we have to verify
R_{J}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
for all
J\in\{0,\dots,4\}^{4}
.

The case
e_{J}=0
and
m_{J}=0
: In this case we have a trivial residuum
R_{J}=0
.

The case
e_{J}=0
and
m_{J}=1
: In this case,
R_{J}=V(q)E_{1}^{1}
, with
V(t):=\big(e_{i}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)\otimes e_{j}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)\big)^{\dagger}\cdot\hat{v}\cdot e_{k}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)\otimes e_{\ell}\left(\vec{t}\ \right)
. Since the
C^{1}
function
V
satisfies
F(0)=0
, we obtain
V(q)E_{1}^{1}=o_{*}\left(\mathbb{T}_{N}\right)
by Lemma
D.2
.

The case
e_{J}=0
and
m_{J}=2
:
In this case
R_{J}=V(q)E_{2}^{1}
, with
